People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Like I still get up, I go to work, I deal with systems issues all day—actually there was this server migration last week that was a complete mess because nobody documented the dependencies—sorry. I guess what I’m saying is, I’ve been bothered and angry and preoccupied, but I’m not sure I’d describe it the way you asked.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your father’s death was handled properly, would you believe it?\n\nPatient: If it was actually clear proof, I mean… I’d look at it. I’m not unreasonable. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. But I haven’t seen that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I guess what I’m saying is, I’ve been bothered and angry and preoccupied, but I’m not sure I’d describe it the way you asked.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your father’s death was handled properly, would you believe it?\n\nPatient: If it was actually clear proof, I mean… I’d look at it. I’m not unreasonable. But people keep saying “clear” when what they mean is the hospital records and those donation emails line up if you don’t look too closely.\n\nWhen I compare the timestamps, and there are missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly don’t want to answer basic questions—sorry, it reminds me of work, where someone says a log is clean but the audit trail has these little gaps—anyway, to me that doesn’t feel handled properly. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. But I haven’t seen that.\n\nDoctor: Before your father died, did you often feel that other people were hiding things from you or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Before? Not really, no. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. That’s when it started feeling like there were things being hidden.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe for certain that someone hid the truth about your father’s death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.

It’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.

I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. That’s when it started feeling like there were things being hidden.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that someone hid the truth about your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Not really in the same way. I mean, at work people can be evasive sometimes—IT departments are like that, everyone says “it’s in the ticket” when it clearly isn’t—but I don’t think that’s the same thing.\n\nWhat I keep coming back to is my father’s death. That’s when it started feeling like there were things being hidden.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that someone hid the truth about your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Not really in the same way. I mean, at work people can be evasive sometimes—IT departments are like that, everyone says “it’s in the ticket” when it clearly isn’t—but I don’t think that’s the same thing.\n\nWhat I keep coming back to is my father’s death. Everyone keeps saying the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and then certain community members avoid my questions… that’s the part that’s obvious to me. Someone is hiding what really happened there. Not, like, the whole world is after me. It’s that situation.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about your father’s death caused serious problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I mean… serious problems? I wouldn’t say serious in the sense that I can’t function. I still go to work, I handle my projects, I pay my bills, keep my normal routine. I’m not, you know, falling apart day to day.\n\nIt’s more that things get tense when I bring up my questions about my father’s records. With family, or some people in the community, they get uncomfortable or shut it down, and then suddenly I’m the difficult one. Which is frustrating, because in my job if something doesn’t add up, you’re supposed to ask questions. But outside of that, I’m still doing what I need to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It’s more like—I wish I’d known what to question earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? I don’t think so. I mean, not like… if you mean seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, no.\n\nSometimes I notice details other people miss, especially in documents or timestamps, but that’s not “seeing things,” that’s just paying attention. My coworkers joke that I can spot a misconfigured field from across the room, which—well, that’s because I’ve spent years staring at dashboards. But actual things other people couldn’t see? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that someone hid the truth about your father’s death come on suddenly?\n\nPatient: Not suddenly, exactly. It’s been… maybe about nine months now. It started after my father died, when I was going through his records and old messages and trying to organize everything.\n\nAt first it was just practical stuff, like sorting files, passwords, paperwork—honestly, I had a whole spreadsheet because that’s just how I deal with things—but then certain things didn’t line up, and I kept coming back to them. So no, it wasn’t like I woke up one morning with this thought out of nowhere. It built up during that period after he died.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you still find yourself yearning intensely for your father or feeling preoccupied with missing him every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I miss him, sure. He was my dad. But “yearning intensely every day” sounds a little dramatic to me, or maybe I’m not understanding the phrasing.

Some days I think about him because something reminds me—like I’ll see some old technical manual or hear a phrase he used to say, and then my mind goes sideways into, you know, how people keep records, how families keep different versions of the same story. My uncle still has boxes of stuff in his garage, completely unorganized, which drives me crazy because if you don’t label things then later everyone acts like the missing pieces were never there.

But missing him every single day in that way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it like that. It’s more complicated than just missing him.

So… I don’t think I’m hearing voices, no.\n\nDoctor: Since your father died, have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’ve been upset, obviously, and frustrated because when you’re trying to get straight answers and people keep acting like the paperwork is clean when it isn’t, it wears on you. \n\nBut “depressed” is kind of a specific word, right? Like I still get up, I go to work, I deal with systems issues all day—actually there was this server migration last week that was a complete mess because nobody documented the dependencies—sorry. I’m not unreasonable. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. But I haven’t seen that.\n\nDoctor: Before your father died, did you often feel that other people were hiding things from you or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Before? Not really, no. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Everyone keeps saying the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and then certain community members avoid my questions… that’s the part that’s obvious to me. It built up during that period after he died.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find yourself yearning intensely for your father or feeling preoccupied with missing him every day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I miss him, sure. He was my dad. But “yearning intensely every day” sounds a little dramatic to me, or maybe I’m not understanding the phrasing.\n\nSome days I think about him because something reminds me—like I’ll see some old technical manual or hear a phrase he used to say, and then my mind goes sideways into, you know, how people keep records, how families keep different versions of the same story. My uncle still has boxes of stuff in his garage, completely unorganized, which drives me crazy because if you don’t label things then later everyone acts like the missing pieces were never there.\n\nBut missing him every single day in that way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it like that. It’s more complicated than just missing him.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince this started, have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you could hardly keep up with them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Racing? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I do think through things a lot, especially when there are inconsistencies, but that’s sort of how my job trained me to be—like if a system throws one weird error, you don’t just ignore it, you check the logs, the timestamps, the dependencies.

But thoughts going so fast I couldn’t keep up with them… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more like I get stuck on a thread and keep following it, not like everything is speeding past me.

It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. But I haven’t seen that.\n\nDoctor: Before your father died, did you often feel that other people were hiding things from you or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Before? Not really, no. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Not really in the same way. That’s a heavier word. I mostly feel like there are unanswered parts, and people want me to stop looking at them. I’m not saying I caused anything. So no, it wasn’t like I woke up one morning with this thought out of nowhere. It built up during that period after he died.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find yourself yearning intensely for your father or feeling preoccupied with missing him every day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I miss him, sure. He was my dad. But “yearning intensely every day” sounds a little dramatic to me, or maybe I’m not understanding the phrasing.\n\nSome days I think about him because something reminds me—like I’ll see some old technical manual or hear a phrase he used to say, and then my mind goes sideways into, you know, how people keep records, how families keep different versions of the same story. My uncle still has boxes of stuff in his garage, completely unorganized, which drives me crazy because if you don’t label things then later everyone acts like the missing pieces were never there.\n\nBut missing him every single day in that way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it like that. It’s more complicated than just missing him.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you could hardly keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Racing? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I do think through things a lot, especially when there are inconsistencies, but that’s sort of how my job trained me to be—like if a system throws one weird error, you don’t just ignore it, you check the logs, the timestamps, the dependencies.\n\nBut thoughts going so fast I couldn’t keep up with them… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more like I get stuck on a thread and keep following it, not like everything is speeding past me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave other people told you that your speech or thoughts have been hard to follow or seemed jumbled?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. Not in those words, anyway.

Sometimes people say I “over-explain” things, especially at work, because I’ll start with the main point and then realize there are three pieces of context they need or they’ll misunderstand it. Like with my dad’s records, if you don’t explain the sequence, people jump to the wrong conclusion. But hard to follow or jumbled… I haven’t really been told that.

I mean, my sister has said, “Can you just get to the point?” but that’s different, right? That’s more impatience than me not making sense.

Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life?   },

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because people keep acting like I’m making something out of nothing, but I’m not. It’s about my father’s death. I’ve been looking at the hospital records and some donation emails, and everyone says they all line up, but when I compare the timestamps and the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly avoid my questions… it’s obvious to me someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like a lot, but I work with systems, I notice patterns. Like even at my job, small mismatches usually mean something got changed or someone doesn’t want you to see the full trail. So I guess I’m here because no one will take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: I mean… no, not like a voice talking to me in an empty room, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll hear people talking in the hallway at work and I’ll wonder if it’s about me, but that’s different, that’s just—well, in offices people act like everything’s normal and then messages disappear or suddenly nobody remembers a conversation. So… I don’t think I’m hearing voices, no.\n\nDoctor: Since your father died, have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’ve been upset, obviously, and frustrated because when you’re trying to get straight answers and people keep acting like the paperwork is clean when it isn’t, it wears on you. \n\nBut “depressed” is kind of a specific word, right? Like I still get up, I go to work, I deal with systems issues all day—actually there was this server migration last week that was a complete mess because nobody documented the dependencies—sorry. I guess what I’m saying is, I’ve been bothered and angry and preoccupied, but I’m not sure I’d describe it the way you asked.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your father’s death was handled properly, would you believe it?\n\nPatient: If it was actually clear proof, I mean… I’d look at it. I’m not unreasonable. But people keep saying “clear” when what they mean is the hospital records and those donation emails line up if you don’t look too closely.\n\nWhen I compare the timestamps, and there are missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly don’t want to answer basic questions—sorry, it reminds me of work, where someone says a log is clean but the audit trail has these little gaps—anyway, to me that doesn’t feel handled properly. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. But I haven’t seen that.\n\nDoctor: Before your father died, did you often feel that other people were hiding things from you or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Before? Not really, no. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. That’s when it started feeling like there were things being hidden.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that someone hid the truth about your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Not really in the same way. I mean, at work people can be evasive sometimes—IT departments are like that, everyone says “it’s in the ticket” when it clearly isn’t—but I don’t think that’s the same thing.\n\nWhat I keep coming back to is my father’s death. Everyone keeps saying the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and then certain community members avoid my questions… that’s the part that’s obvious to me. Someone is hiding what really happened there. Not, like, the whole world is after me. It’s that situation.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about your father’s death caused serious problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I mean… serious problems? I wouldn’t say serious in the sense that I can’t function. I still go to work, I handle my projects, I pay my bills, keep my normal routine. I’m not, you know, falling apart day to day.\n\nIt’s more that things get tense when I bring up my questions about my father’s records. With family, or some people in the community, they get uncomfortable or shut it down, and then suddenly I’m the difficult one. Which is frustrating, because in my job if something doesn’t add up, you’re supposed to ask questions. But outside of that, I’m still doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel responsible for your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Responsible? I don’t… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I keep thinking there were things I should’ve asked sooner, or looked at more closely, you know? Like when you miss a warning sign in a system and later everyone says it was “within normal parameters,” but you remember something felt off.\n\nBut responsible for his death… I don’t know. That’s a heavier word. I mostly feel like there are unanswered parts, and people want me to stop looking at them. I’m not saying I caused anything. It’s more like—I wish I’d known what to question earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? I don’t think so. I mean, not like… if you mean seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, no.\n\nSometimes I notice details other people miss, especially in documents or timestamps, but that’s not “seeing things,” that’s just paying attention. My coworkers joke that I can spot a misconfigured field from across the room, which—well, that’s because I’ve spent years staring at dashboards. But actual things other people couldn’t see? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that someone hid the truth about your father’s death come on suddenly?\n\nPatient: Not suddenly, exactly. It’s been… maybe about nine months now. It started after my father died, when I was going through his records and old messages and trying to organize everything.\n\nAt first it was just practical stuff, like sorting files, passwords, paperwork—honestly, I had a whole spreadsheet because that’s just how I deal with things—but then certain things didn’t line up, and I kept coming back to them. I’m not unreasonable. But people keep saying “clear” when what they mean is the hospital records and those donation emails line up if you don’t look too closely.\n\nWhen I compare the timestamps, and there are missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly don’t want to answer basic questions—sorry, it reminds me of work, where someone says a log is clean but the audit trail has these little gaps—anyway, to me that doesn’t feel handled properly. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess… I’d believe real proof. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who notices when details don’t line up—at work that’s basically my job, you know, finding the missing piece in a system—but this particular thing, the feeling that people were keeping things from me, that really started after my father died.\n\nIt’s been… about nine months now, I guess. Since I started going through his records and old messages and trying to make sense of what happened. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or deceive you in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Other parts of my life? Not really in the same way. I mean, at work people can be evasive sometimes—IT departments are like that, everyone says “it’s in the ticket” when it clearly isn’t—but I don’t think that’s the same thing.\n\nWhat I keep coming back to is my father’s death. Everyone keeps saying the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and then certain community members avoid my questions… that’s the part that’s obvious to me. Someone is hiding what really happened there. Not, like, the whole world is after me. It’s that situation.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief about your father’s death caused serious problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I mean… serious problems? I wouldn’t say serious in the sense that I can’t function. I still go to work, I handle my projects, I pay my bills, keep my normal routine. I’m not, you know, falling apart day to day.\n\nIt’s more that things get tense when I bring up my questions about my father’s records. With family, or some people in the community, they get uncomfortable or shut it down, and then suddenly I’m the difficult one. Which is frustrating, because in my job if something doesn’t add up, you’re supposed to ask questions. But outside of that, I’m still doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel responsible for your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Responsible? I don’t… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I keep thinking there were things I should’ve asked sooner, or looked at more closely, you know? Like when you miss a warning sign in a system and later everyone says it was “within normal parameters,” but you remember something felt off.\n\nBut responsible for his death… I don’t know. That’s a heavier word. I mostly feel like there are unanswered parts, and people want me to stop looking at them. I’m not saying I caused anything. It’s more like—I wish I’d known what to question earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? I don’t think so. I mean, not like… if you mean seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, no.\n\nSometimes I notice details other people miss, especially in documents or timestamps, but that’s not “seeing things,” that’s just paying attention. My coworkers joke that I can spot a misconfigured field from across the room, which—well, that’s because I’ve spent years staring at dashboards. But actual things other people couldn’t see? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that someone hid the truth about your father’s death come on suddenly?\n\nPatient: Not suddenly, exactly. It’s been… maybe about nine months now. It started after my father died, when I was going through his records and old messages and trying to organize everything.\n\nAt first it was just practical stuff, like sorting files, passwords, paperwork—honestly, I had a whole spreadsheet because that’s just how I deal with things—but then certain things didn’t line up, and I kept coming back to them. So no, it wasn’t like I woke up one morning with this thought out of nowhere. It built up during that period after he died.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find yourself yearning intensely for your father or feeling preoccupied with missing him every day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I miss him, sure. He was my dad. But “yearning intensely every day” sounds a little dramatic to me, or maybe I’m not understanding the phrasing.\n\nSome days I think about him because something reminds me—like I’ll see some old technical manual or hear a phrase he used to say, and then my mind goes sideways into, you know, how people keep records, how families keep different versions of the same story. My uncle still has boxes of stuff in his garage, completely unorganized, which drives me crazy because if you don’t label things then later everyone acts like the missing pieces were never there.\n\nBut missing him every single day in that way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it like that. It’s more complicated than just missing him.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you could hardly keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Racing? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I do think through things a lot, especially when there are inconsistencies, but that’s sort of how my job trained me to be—like if a system throws one weird error, you don’t just ignore it, you check the logs, the timestamps, the dependencies.\n\nBut thoughts going so fast I couldn’t keep up with them… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. With family, or some people in the community, they get uncomfortable or shut it down, and then suddenly I’m the difficult one. Which is frustrating, because in my job if something doesn’t add up, you’re supposed to ask questions. But outside of that, I’m still doing what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel responsible for your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Responsible? I don’t… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I keep thinking there were things I should’ve asked sooner, or looked at more closely, you know? Like when you miss a warning sign in a system and later everyone says it was “within normal parameters,” but you remember something felt off.\n\nBut responsible for his death… I don’t know. That’s a heavier word. I mostly feel like there are unanswered parts, and people want me to stop looking at them. I’m not saying I caused anything. It’s more like—I wish I’d known what to question earlier.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? I don’t think so. I mean, not like… if you mean seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, no.\n\nSometimes I notice details other people miss, especially in documents or timestamps, but that’s not “seeing things,” that’s just paying attention. My coworkers joke that I can spot a misconfigured field from across the room, which—well, that’s because I’ve spent years staring at dashboards. But actual things other people couldn’t see? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that someone hid the truth about your father’s death come on suddenly?\n\nPatient: Not suddenly, exactly. It’s been… maybe about nine months now. It started after my father died, when I was going through his records and old messages and trying to organize everything.\n\nAt first it was just practical stuff, like sorting files, passwords, paperwork—honestly, I had a whole spreadsheet because that’s just how I deal with things—but then certain things didn’t line up, and I kept coming back to them. So no, it wasn’t like I woke up one morning with this thought out of nowhere. It built up during that period after he died.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find yourself yearning intensely for your father or feeling preoccupied with missing him every day?\n\nPatient: I mean… I miss him, sure. He was my dad. But “yearning intensely every day” sounds a little dramatic to me, or maybe I’m not understanding the phrasing.\n\nSome days I think about him because something reminds me—like I’ll see some old technical manual or hear a phrase he used to say, and then my mind goes sideways into, you know, how people keep records, how families keep different versions of the same story. My uncle still has boxes of stuff in his garage, completely unorganized, which drives me crazy because if you don’t label things then later everyone acts like the missing pieces were never there.\n\nBut missing him every single day in that way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it like that. It’s more complicated than just missing him.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you could hardly keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Racing? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I do think through things a lot, especially when there are inconsistencies, but that’s sort of how my job trained me to be—like if a system throws one weird error, you don’t just ignore it, you check the logs, the timestamps, the dependencies.\n\nBut thoughts going so fast I couldn’t keep up with them… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more like I get stuck on a thread and keep following it, not like everything is speeding past me.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or thoughts have been hard to follow or seemed jumbled?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in those words, anyway.\n\nSometimes people say I “over-explain” things, especially at work, because I’ll start with the main point and then realize there are three pieces of context they need or they’ll misunderstand it. Like with my dad’s records, if you don’t explain the sequence, people jump to the wrong conclusion. But hard to follow or jumbled… I haven’t really been told that.\n\nI mean, my sister has said, “Can you just get to the point?” but that’s different, right? That’s more impatience than me not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been stretches when you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day for two weeks. I’ve had bad stretches, sure, but “nearly every day” and “at least two weeks” feels like you’re asking me to put a calendar grid over it, and I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nSometimes I get low, especially when things feel… unresolved, I guess. But then work picks up, or I get distracted fixing something, and it’s not the same all day. So I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other beliefs that people were hiding things from you, apart from what happened with your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Not really apart from that. I mean, it all kind of connects back to my dad.\n\nIt’s not like I walk around thinking everyone is hiding things from me in general. But with the hospital records and those donation emails, people keep saying, “It lines up, it’s all there,” and when I compare the timestamps and the missing attachments and then certain people in the community suddenly don’t answer direct questions… I don’t know how else to read that.\n\nSorry, I know I’m going off a bit. It’s just, if you’ve ever worked with logs, you know when something doesn’t match, you don’t just accept someone saying, “No, it’s fine.” To me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around his death."
  }
]

Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI mean… no, not like a voice talking to me in an empty room, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I’ll hear people talking in the hallway at work and I’ll wonder if it’s about me, but that’s different, that’s just—well, in offices people act like everything’s normal and then messages disappear or suddenly nobody remembers a conversation. So… I don’t think I’m hearing voices, no.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… I do. I mean, people keep telling me the hospital records and those donation emails all match up, like that should just settle it, but when I actually compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community suddenly dodge simple questions—it’s hard not to see it.\n\nIt’s like at work when a system report says everything completed successfully, but the logs have gaps. You don’t just ignore that. So yes, to me it’s obvious someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
]

Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nResponsible? I don’t… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I keep thinking there were things I should’ve asked sooner, or looked at more closely, you know? Like when you miss a warning sign in a system and later everyone says it was “within normal parameters,” but you remember something felt off.\n\nBut responsible for his death… I don’t know. That’s a heavier word. I mostly feel like there are unanswered parts, and people want me to stop looking at them. I’m not saying I caused anything. It’s more like—I wish I’d known what to question earlier.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so. Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.
